ParlaMint-AT_1997-09-18-020-XX-NRSITZ-00084_d7e15035	Ladies and gentlemen! Federal Minister! High house! Article 18 of our Federal Constitution makes it absolutely clear that the entire state administration can only be exercised under the laws. Since 1920 there have been discussions in the executive field precisely on this provision, because the standard density in the executive field was much lower than in all other areas. For many years there has been the argument that the safety executive needs a corresponding freedom of movement in order to fulfil its tasks. Finally, there has been a clear commitment in this context in the 1980s. Article 18 applies to the entire executive – to the security executive, but also to the military. At the end of the 1980s, therefore, we developed the "Political Power Act" in long, disfficulty and complex discussions – as it was said at the beginning phase – the Security Police Act, which was passed in 1991 and entered into force in 1993. Ladies and gentlemen! My group is simply saying that what applies to internal security is a clear legal determination, what the executive is allowed to do and, e contrario, what it is not allowed to do, of course also has to be applied to the military sector. [[Congratulations with the SPÖ.]] Therefore, the first step was taken at the beginning of the nineties with the decision-making of the police power law, and the next step now has to be taken with the decision-making of a military power law – or call it military law any time you want. This is our objective, and we shall pursue it, and we will not pursue this objective only now, because we can point out that we have pursued this objective in the past. When the difficult discussions in this regard – and they will be no less difficult than the discussions in the police sector – have given us a taste for slow development, we have applications in this House about a year ago for a service law, a small The area of this military security area, with which we want to realize exactly what is natural in almost every democratic state – look at the Federal Republic of Germany! – namely that there is a clear determination of the powers which the military actually gives to such services, which in the meantime are completely unjustly called "secret services". Ladies and gentlemen! Mr Wabl said he was afraid of shooting. I understand this because this usually means your own shots in the knee. In this context, I can tell you that not every paper above the "Bundesgesetz" is actually published in the Federal Law. I would like to say a few words to you: I am grateful to the Ministry of Defence that the work has begun, but that what is now available is, at best, a first, provisional draft discussion. On the one hand, we from the Socialist Group have initiated the work and encouraged the Ministry of Defence to take up these work, but we have very clear criteria in this regard. First criterion: The rule of law must also be fully implemented in this area. Secondly, The Federal Army and its organizations have to confine themselves to military tasks, which means that there should be no security police tasks with the Federal Army, but only the executive. [[Congratulations with the SPÖ.]] We will probably also need, in this draft, a differentiation of the powers for the use and for the case of peace. In addition, we will have to make a clear distinction between army administration and the actual military part of the federal army – even though this is still missing, but drafts and only fairly raw designs are somewhat imperfect. We will also need a corresponding system for the services. We will ensure that the results of the studies, which are to the greatest extent based on open sources, are available to the entire Federal Government and not just part of it. We will have to insist that the powers of the federal army and its institutions are valued. And we will also have to have restrictions on the scope in some areas, both in terms of subject matter and in terms of personnel. As a lawyer, formulations such as the quite global "precaution against subversive forms of threat" show me a need to formulate more concrete, clear and, above all, militaryly related. The federal army has tasks – I fully agree – but it has to carry out these tasks, like any other area, in cooperation with the relevant parts of the security executive. [[Congratulations with the SPÖ.]] We will have far-reaching fundamental rights protection in this context, and we will also have information for citizens. My group, ladies and gentlemen, has long been working on it. I recognise once again the willingness of the German army and the Ministry of Defence to enter into this discussion. Ladies and gentlemen of the Green Group! I can also tell you: For us, this is a topic both before the 5. ==References====External links== October. That is why the fight against organised crime will have top priority for me in the next future as an interior minister, but also for all of us, I believe. We must also be aware, however, that we have no chance, by means of conventional means and by traditional methods, of becoming even nearer to this organised crime. As important and necessary as I believe that more guards, more police officers and gendarmen are on the streets, I also believe that the implementation of these necessary and important demands will not be achieved so that organised crime in Austria or Europe is somehow halted. This requires other measures, which I would like to present very briefly and superficially. First of all, I would like to thank Parliament very much for the opportunity of all the Members of the various political parties who have given the Austrian executive the opportunity to use new arrest methods in the near future. These new arrest methods serve almost exclusively to control organised crime in Austria. Whether this is a noise attack, whether it's a grid arrest, whether it's a hidden investigation, or whether it's a witness protection, all these are measures that I believe will give us the opportunity to take action against organised crime. In addition, I would like to announce that from October this year we will create our own OK card and file in Austria, and I hope that this will also allow us to take greater action against organised crime. We will relieve Austrian criminal officials of the fight against small crime in the next future. Finally, we cannot resist organised crime unless we cooperate internationally in the fight against crime. ParlaMint-AT_1997-09-18-020-XX-NRSITZ-00084_d7e18785	Mr President. My venerations Ladies and gentlemen! The focus of justice policy on internal security comes from the need for a determined fight against serious and organised crime on the one hand, but also from the need for the most meaningful perpetrator and sacrifice-oriented reactions in the area of mass everyday crime on the other. To the same extent, we need to ensure a rational execution, which is consistent with the safe custody of the criminal as well as with his optimal preparation for the release and reintegration into society. In everything we do in order to ensure internal security, we have a legal standard which, at least, meets the requirements of the European Convention on Human Rights and the case law of the Strasbourg bodies. These are indisputable objectives that sometimes conflict with each other. A modern judicial policy must therefore constantly make a fresh effort to reconcile these different objectives. As far as the fight against serious crime and organised crime is concerned, I would like to point out once again – I am sorry that my predecessor is just so busy – that their fight requires not only an extension of the legal and factual instruments available to the security and judicial authorities, but also an overall strategy with elements of technical, organisational, social and legal, especially administrative, prevention. This means that, although new forms of criminality are to be combated, they must not only be used in the fields of material and formal criminal law. Nevertheless, it was of course necessary that the fight against organised crime has received massive support from the legislator in recent years. I would remind you of the new criminal offences against money laundering and criminal organisation, as well as the new provisions on the levying, collection and decay of criminal profits, i.e. measures to be taken by international organised crime in its nerve, in its financial centre. I am not surprised by the fact that the 1995 Security Report shows only a small number of convictions according to the money laundering stock, when one considers that it has only been in force since 1994 and that only legal convictions are found in this report. However, I fear that since then it may not have become much more, which I would like to say above all, that these cases are very often foreign-related, and that legal aid is not only long-term, but also often administrative, but also – in the absence of comparable penalties – legal difficulties, but fortunately, at least the persecution of the criminal offence for money laundering remains. I would also like to remind you of the federal law adopted before the summer, which introduced special investigation measures to combat organised crime in StPO. In the course of the preparation of the law, the area of justice has always been a careful balance between improving police efficiency and ensuring the greatest possible respect for the fundamental and freedom rights of the individual. The law, in particular the multi-stage measures against any possible abuse, reflects this effort. It was therefore my particular concern to be able to appoint, in good time before the entry into force of the provisions on the approximation of data at the beginning of October, renowned and highly qualified persons as legal guardians, appointed as an independent procedural party for both the control of law and proportionality and the monitoring of the implementation of the special investigation measures. Ladies and gentlemen! International cooperation is of particular importance in combating the increasingly cross-border organised crime. That is why the Member States of the European Union have made greater efforts in the field of justice and home affairs cooperation and have drawn up a comprehensive action plan to combat and prevent organised crime, which was adopted by the last European Council in Amsterdam. This document covers not only 15 policy guidelines to combat organised crime, but also a detailed, 30 recommendations comprehensive, time-consuming action plan addressed to the Commission, the Council and the Member States themselves and addressed issues of prevention, the effectiveness of the legal instrument, the improvement of practical cooperation between judicial authorities, police and customs, the extension of Europol and measures to be taken by organised crime in its financial centre. The Federal Ministry of Justice is currently preparing for the implementation of several acts which have already been completed under the third pillar on the protection of the Community's financial interests, fraud prevention and corruption. In order to implement these conventions, the State of fraud shall be supplemented by all types of subsidy abuse and the existing criminal offences against civil servants shall also be declared applicable to the Community officials and to officials of the other Member States. Ladies and gentlemen! As far as dealing with everyday crime is concerned, I would also like to announce here that it should be improved in a modern way and that the possibilities should be extended. This includes the fact-finding of the out-of-court settlement, which has been successfully implemented for a decade, as well as the as soon as possible law-making of a criminal procedure currently under review in 1998. In the future, the diverging provided for in this Directive will allow for a simpler but better coordinated handling of less serious infringements of less dangerous offenders. The main focus is on the compensation of damages and the other interests of the victims, to which an ideal satisfaction belongs. With this StPO-Novelle, justice is not only in line with the unanimous resolution of the National Council of July 1994, but I think that those who know the draft will be right in giving me more to the victims of crimes than any judicial law in the second Republic. And in the last decade, we have just passed few judicial laws that have brought about greater consideration of the interests of victims and a significant improvement in the situation of those injured by crime. However, regardless of this step-by-step stronger victim orientation of criminal law and criminal proceedings, the state aid measures to victims of crime still seem unsatisfying in the treatment of material and immaterial consequences. However, the possibilities of criminal proceedings are limited. Rather, there will be a need for determined joint efforts by the federal and state governments to set up and promote more victim assistance and victim advice facilities. The first step could be to strengthen the coordination and networking of existing institutions, in particular in the fields of youth welfare and the advice of victims of sexual offences, which may be devoted to legal and psychosocial advice of victims of crime and to the follow-up of proceedings by a trusted person with the necessary contacts with the courts and prosecutors. In this context, the draft criminal procedure for 1998, which is under review, provides that the judicial body shall support the additional revenue to be obtained from the payment of fines to be made in the distribution proceedings. In particular, support will be given to those who are devoted to the care of minor victims or persons who have been injured in their sexual sphere. Our further reforms in the field of victim protection range from the follow-up of proceedings and advice during and after the end of the criminal proceedings to the procedural status of the victim in the overall structure of the criminal proceedings. The related structural issues, which are particularly relevant to the issue of procedural protection and enforcement of victim rights, must be dealt with in the light of the now due re-design of the criminal procedure. Within the framework of this project, which I am pursuing with the highest priority – a draft discussion is to be presented about the end of 1997/98 – we believe that further improvement of the status of the injured person will be achieved by granting further information and party rights to the victim – beyond the rights of the private party under the current legal situation. A few words on another reform project in the field of justice. Taking into account shocking cases of sexual abuse, in particular of children, not only domestically, abroad, but also in view of the need for reform, which is at least going back to the last major change in sexual criminal law in 1989, I have convened at the beginning of the year a multidisciplinary working group whose aim is to develop a basis for a comprehensive renewal of sexual criminal law. More generally, sexual self-determination rather than a no longer modern moral concept is to be applied. In particular, an association of the crimes committed by sleeping in and other acts of fornication to the unmistakable, associated with penetration, is intended with a view to ensuring that such penalties will also be more severe. In addition, accompanying areas, such as the question of limitation in the event of sexual abuse, are also discussed. When considering all for and against, it seems to me that there is a great deal to be talk of extending this limitation period beyond reaching the age of old age. Finally, a few words on crime victims and fear of crime in international comparison. The final report of an international crime victim survey carried out in 1996 has just reached us. If the results are summarized, it can be seen that Austria has a relatively low crime rate and, together with Finland, the lowest number of victims of crime. Similarly, the fear of the respondents to become victims of a delicate attack in the coming year is similar. I believe that, in order to ensure fair and complete information to the public, such good news must also be proclaimed and that they must also be included in the considerations on a balanced criminal law policy. – Thank you for your attention.

ParlaMint-AT_1997-09-18-020-XX-NRSITZ-00084_d7e20858	Mr President. My Dear Ones Ladies and gentlemen! I would like to draw attention to what Mrs Haidlmayr and Mr Schwemlein said, namely the value and importance of civil service. I believe that there has been a marked turnaround in this area in recent years and that the civil servant in the population is much more recognized than it has been in the past. A recent study from October 1996, conducted by IMAS, shows that 77 percent of the Austrian population are very positive about civil service, 44 percent believe that civil service is a very good institution, and only 13 percent of the Austrian population are against civil service. In my opinion, the fact that civil servants are particularly motivated is also very important. The civil servants in their overwhelming majority are very involved in their activities and appreciate this activity very positively. I also believe that, in the course of his work, the civil servant has gained a very useful experience for his further life, that civil service provides a good insight into the social problems of our country and contributes to the fact that civil servants can better cope with future difficult situations in life. Ladies and gentlemen! Mr Scheibner has raised a few points to which I would like to address briefly. First: I do not see the development of civil servants as problematic. The development was perhaps problematic for the Austrian federal army a few years ago, but now, as I think, there is a reasonable relationship. In 1990, there were some 41 000 suitable military servants, and I am always based only on adequate military officers and also on recognised civilian servants, not on those who apply. In 1990, we had 41 000 decent military officers and 2,500 recognized civilian servants. In 1993 and 1994 it looked quite different: As a result, the number of suitable military servants has fallen to 35 000 to 36 000, but the number of recognised civilian servants has increased to more than 13 000, i.e. the disproportionate relationship has been enormous. In 1996, we had 35 000 suitable military servants and 6,300 recognized civilian servants. – In my opinion, this relationship is also acceptable to the Austrian Federal Army and ensures that the Austrian Federal Army can continue to fulfil its obligations in the future. [[Abg. Scheibner: Fassleven says he needs 34,000 groundmen!]] The development in this year is similar. We will probably receive between 6 000 and 6 500 civil service applications this year, about 6 200 to 6 300 will be recognized. For me as an interior minister, of course, the problem is – I do not want to dispute – that we have received more and more civilian services in recent years – it is already over 700 – and that these civilian services also need more civilian services. This year alone, I believe, we could accommodate more than 9 200 civilians in the facilities. It is, of course, necessary and important that civil servants be more allocated in the future. We had 6 800 in the past year, 6 400 in the year before, 6 400 in this year we will allocate 6 400, and next year it will be almost 7 000, so the number will have increased. I must confirm the fear you have expressed, Mr Scheibner: According to my project, the civil service law will be amended again. It is already a change that I have made in my opinion, and this change takes into account my view that every civil service organisation should also make its financial contribution. At present, this is very, very different. There are organisations that do not have to pay any compensation at all, while others have to pay an amount of between 1 500 and 7 000 S. I do not think that this is justified, and I have therefore given an opinion on this subject, and I hope that you in Parliament will agree that every legal entity will have to make a minimum contribution in the future. The funds provided for this will be used, among other things, to enable more civil servants to be allocated to civil service facilities and to satisfy the needs of each institution. They have criticised the question of "Radio Mozambique/Foreign Service" – I do not necessarily want to defend this because I believe that this is without a doubt a border case. It is not necessarily the task of Austrian civil servants to take part in Radio Mozambique, although I do not consider this project to be so bad from the democratic point of view – I have asked myself. That is why, together with the Ministry of Foreign Affairs, discussions will be held in the next few days so that the conditions for the recognition of civil servants abroad are made clearer and more precise. I would also like to maintain and defend that civil servants will continue to be used for road safety in the future. In any case, this is a very small number, to say it precisely: 3.1% of all civil servants are active in the field of transport safety, particularly in the field of school road safety. In view of the fact that we have increasing numbers of accidents and increasing numbers of fatalities and that many children are also killed in road transport, I think this is an important and necessary work. They have indirectly or directly referred to the falling number of volunteers in the various organizations as "evil evidence" for the government or for society as a whole. I mean, this is a social phenomenon that we are exposed to. Each one of us knows – there are a number of Members who are volunteering in various organizations – that there is no longer any access to organizations such as the Red Cross, to Blue Light organizations, to firearms and similar organizations to the extent that this was the case before. On this point, I believe, we are in common demand, not just the Federal Government. We must all achieve that more young people, but also adults, are willing to work for such organizations. I would like to say to what Mr Maitz said about the convocation: I see it just as you do. It is also a concern of us, not only to save costs, but also because I believe it makes more sense for young people to do their federal or civil service as soon as possible. After completing a higher education course, just before the start of the career course, this is always a problem. I would like to take the criticism of Mrs Haidlmayr very seriously. I am willing to work out common solutions in this regard, how these overlap periods can be changed. We currently have three convocation dates. It is certainly not a problem to create a fourth appointment date in addition, so that the overlap is very low for such organisations. I would ask Mrs Haidlmayr to talk to me about this so that we can find a common solution. The problem of housing communities is also clear to me. However, this applies not only to civilians, but also to federal soldiers. It is laid down by law that a person will receive compensation for an apartment only if, on the basis of a contract which he has concluded before the arrival of his Federal Army or civil service, he can prove that he has actual costs for an apartment, because the risk of manipulation and the risk of abuse is of course very strong if there is no written agreement. That is why I believe that we must press from the legislator for a corresponding regulation. If there is a clear written agreement and there is evidence that the costs involved are to be borne, this is not a problem either. Ladies and gentlemen! Finally, I would like to say something about the comments made by Mr Kier: I would have liked it if he had said that, contrary to the security report, the civil service report had been properly and in time. For the civil service report is already available to Parliament in April 1997, even if it is unfortunately being dealt with only today. I hope that this will also be the case in the future in the Security Report. On the issue of the declining number of civil servants: I don't see a problem there. I think we can update the number of 14 000 over the next few years. The delay has significantly reduced the number of civil servants. In the next few years, however, I would like to call more civilians, but, as I believe, we will be able to achieve this number. No one should fall by rust against his will and not be able to support his civil service. I would like to give this right to everyone, since I believe that there is no problem in this regard. In this sense, I thank everyone for their comments and believe that civil service in Austria has become an institution that finds great recognition.

They can only be assumed that they have not read the law and the comments that exist and that they have not taken account of the processes which led to the Commission's being drawn up. This includes the introduction of a limitation provision into the relevant legislation. In fact, the law is strict today, even if, ten years after he has disarmed, a basic defender continues to complain about any action affecting him during his service. That is why a limitation period of about one year is included in this respect. It is also necessary, however, to create a border of luggage. We ourselves have become accustomed to speaking in the Judicature of the fact that there is a degree of inadequacy in the case of objects of appeal which lacks legal relevance. This means, as we all know, that, naturally, the army cannot always do the same because of its task and design as in a folk school – even if it is sometimes the same as in a folk school. It must be, however, that true bagatellan occasions are not actually answered with some sort of guilt. It must be so that one can go the narrow grate between schikane, injustice or injustice on the one hand, and ridiculous complaints dealing with small things, on the other hand, without slipping down on one side or the other. That is why my appeal, my request to the legislator, is: Please introduce, on the next occasion, when it comes to the Wehrgesetz, the limitation on the one hand, and on the other hand, a border of luggage into the relevant regulations, so that we do not need to shoot cannons in order to comply with the will of the legislator. A material detail as long as the light shines red here: It is still true that a full-length ground defense officer is liable if he is negligently – even if with a certain degree of negligence – something happens to him that causes a damage to an object, a weapon or a vehicle. The basic defender, who deserves virtually nothing, often has to do damage to tens of thousands of schilling. This cannot change the fact that sometimes the financial procuratorship on behalf of the Ministry of Finance closes comparisons, so that "only" - under quotation marks - 30 000 S or amounts can be paid in a similar amount. We represent the position: If someone serves the army – and doesn't always like it – and then something doesn't happen to him deafly, but negligently, it just happened and should pay the Republic – and not the affected person himself. That would also be a wish for the legislator.

ParlaMint-AT_1997-09-18-020-XX-NRSITZ-00084_d7e23133	The debate has not been called for nor has it been decided. We shall therefore proceed to the vote on this request. I ask those ladies and gentlemen who are in favour of this request to have a corresponding sign of approval. – This is the minority. The application is rejected. Inflow
ParlaMint-AT_1997-09-18-020-XX-NRSITZ-00084_d7e23169	I would also like to say that at today's sitting, the self-employed were brought in applications 555/A to 563/A. The next meeting of the National Council is scheduled for Friday, 19. September 1997, 9 a.m. This meeting will begin with a Question Time.
